Purity of generalized affine Springer fibers from generic planar curve singularities
Taiwang Deng, Tao Su

Abstract
. We prove the cohomological purity of punctual Hilbert schemes of points on generic irreducible planar curve singularities, by constructing an explicit affine paving. Via their identification with generalized G ​ L N GL_{N} -affine Springer fibers attached to the direct sum of the adjoint and standard representations, this establishes a new case of the purity conjecture for generalized affine Springer fibers. The combinatorics of the paving - cell indices and dimensions - are controlled by ( d ​ n , d ​ m ) (dn,dm) -Dyck paths extending results of Gorsky-Mazin-Oblomkov on compactified Jacobians. As a byproduct, we also give a simpler proof of their bijection between admissible ( d ​ n , d ​ m ) (dn,dm) -invariant subsets and ( d ​ n , d ​ m ) (dn,dm) -Dyck paths.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 14H20, 14C05; Secondary: 05E14, 05E10

1.
Introduction

Affine Springer fibers were introduced by Kazhdan-Lusztig
[
21
]
in their study of orbital integrals on reductive groups over local fields, relating orbital integrals to point counts of affine Springer fibers.
In
[
11
]
, Goresky-Kottwitz-MacPherson formulated a
purity conjecture
for affine Springer fibers and, under this assumption, established the function field analogue of the fundamental lemma
[
24
]
in the unramified case, the latter being a combinatorial identity appears in the work
[
23
]
,
[
22
]
and
[
24
]
, and plays a prominant role in the classical Langlands program.
They further verified the conjecture in the equivalued case
[
12
]
.
Via the Grothendieck-Lefschetz trace formula, purity ensures that orbital integrals can be expressed in terms of the cohomology of affine Springer fibers.
The general purity conjecture, however, remains open.
It was precisely this obstacle that Ngô overcame in his proof of the fundamental lemma
[
28
]
, by passing to the global setting of the Hitchin fibration and applying the decomposition and support theorems.

As a natural extension, Goresky-Kottwitz-MacPherson
[
12
]
introduced
generalized affine Springer fibers (GASF)
by replacing the adjoint representation with an arbitrary finite-dimensional representation of a reductive group.
More recently, these varieties became prominent as natural geometric sources for realizing representations
[
19
,
10
]
of (quantized) Coulomb branch algebras
[
3
]
.
It is expected that the purity conjecture extends to this broader setting:

Conjecture 1.1
.

Under mild conditions, the (co)homology of a generalized affine Springer fiber is pure.

Partial progress has been made in various settings (see
[
6
,
16
,
7
,
13
]
), but the general case remains wide open.
In particular, in
[
16
]
the authors study the GASF for
G=GL_{n}^{\times r}
and the representation
V=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbf{k}^{n},\mathbf{k}^{n})^{\oplus r}\oplus\mathbf{k}^{n}
.
They show that in this case the GASF corresponds to the compositional Hilbert scheme of quasi-homogeneous planar curve singularities (
\{y^{n}-x^{m}=0\}
)
[
16
, Prop. 8.9]
, and prove that it admits an affine paving
[
16
, Prop. 8.12]
.
Finally, we expect the generalized purity conjecture to play a role in the geometry of the relative fundamental lemma, as in the classical case.

Main result

In this article we prove Conjecture
1.1
for a new family of generalized affine Springer fibers arising from
generic irreducible planar curve singularities

(C,0)
.
By
[
10
, Thm. 3.3]
, these fibers are naturally identified with the (punctual) Hilbert schemes
\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\bullet]}(C,0)
.

Our first main result is:

Theorem 1.2

(Cor.
3.11
, Thm.
3.10
)
.

Let
(C,0)
be a
generic
irreducible planar curve singularity:

x=t^{dn},\quad y=t^{dm}+\lambda t^{dm+1}+\text{ h.o.t.},\quad\gcd(n,m)=1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \lambda\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}.

Then

\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\bullet]}(C,0)\cong\mathrm{Spr}_{\mathfrak{v}}
admits an explicit affine paving:

\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)=\sqcup_{\Delta\in\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm):\penalty\ \tau-e(\Delta)\in\Delta}H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]},\quad H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\dim\Delta-|\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau-e(\Delta))|}.

In particular, its (co)homology is pure.

The relevant terminology will be recalled in the next section.
The construction of our paving extends the approach of
[
13
]
, which established affine pavings and purity for (local) compactified Jacobians of
(C,0)
as above.
In this setting, the correspondence with equivalued affine Springer fibers in
[
12
]
holds precisely when
d=1
, i.e., when the curve singularity is quasi-homogeneous (see Remark
2.15
).

Our second main result gives a new proof of the bijection between
\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
, the set of admissible
(dn,dm)
-invariant subsets, and
\mathcal{Y}(dn,dm)
, the set of
(dn,dm)
-Dyck paths, originally established in
[
13
,
14
]
. See Theorem
3.22
for a precise reformulation and a refinement.
Our constructions of the bijection
\Psi_{d}:\mathcal{Y}(dn,dm)\to\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
(Section
3.3.3
, Proposition
4.4
) and its inverse
\Phi_{d}:\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)\to\mathcal{Y}(dm,dm)
(Proposition
4.3
) are much simpler than the originals, and may be of independent interest.
While a priori it is not clear that the two constructions coincide, we prove that
\Psi_{d}
is compatible with the sweep map (Proposition
4.13
), thereby establishing the equivalence (Remark
4.14
).

Our third result describes the combinatorics of the paving - cell indices and dimensions - in terms of
(dn,dm)
-Dyck paths, generalizing
[
13
]
on compactified Jacobians
\overline{J}(C,0)
. See Proposition
3.24
.

Finally, our original motivation comes from the Oblomkov-Rasmussen-Shende (ORS) conjecture
[
29
,
30
]
and the Cherednik-Danilenko-Philipp (CDP) conjecture
[
4
,
5
]
, which connect enumerate geometry of planar curve singularities with knot invariants and double affine Hecke algebras (DAHA).
Concretely, let
L(q,t,a)
be the
Hilbert
L
-function
of
(C,0)
(Definition
3.30
), the generating function for the cohomology of
\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\bullet]}(C,0)
. The ORS conjecture (see
[
30
, Conj. 2]
or Conjecture
3.31
) identifies
L(q,t,a)
with the HOMFLY-PT homology of the singularity link of
(C,0)
, up to a change of variables.
For generic
(C,0)
, our affine paving allows a computation of
L(q,t,0)
.
Moreover,
L(q,t,0)
encodes the
perverse filtration
on the cohomology of
\overline{J}(C,0)

[
26
,
27
]
.
As a consequence, we compute the perverse filtration for generic
(C,0)
(Cor.
3.36
).

On the other hand, let
\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{mot}}(q,t,a)
denote the
normalized motivic superpolynomial
of
(C,0)
(Definition
3.37
), originating from DAHA representations.
An abridged version of the Cherednik-Danilenko-Philipp conjecture asserts:

Conjecture 1.3

(
[
4
,
5
]
)
.

L(q,t,a)=\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{mot}}(q,t,a)
.

In the setting of a generic irreducible planar curve singularity
(C,0)
, our affine paving reduces the
(q,t)
-version of this conjecture to the following purely combinatorial statement:

Conjecture 1.4

(Conj.
3.39
)
.

(1.1)

(1-t)\sum_{D\in\mathcal{Y}(dn,dm)}\sum_{z\in\overline{D}}q^{|D|-\mathrm{dinv}(D)+\hat{\epsilon}_{D}(z)}t^{|D|+\hat{\mathrm{rk}}_{D}(z)}=\sum_{D\in\mathcal{Y}(dn,dm)}q^{\mathrm{co}\text{-}\mathrm{dinv}(D)}t^{\mathrm{co}\text{-}\mathrm{dinv}(D)+\delta-|D|}.

See Section
3.4
for details.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Planar curve singularities

Let
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{C}
be the base field.
Consider a reduced irreducible planar curve singularity
(C,0)
, which after an analytic coordinate change, admits a parametrization of the form (see
[
15
, I.Cor. 3.8]
):

(2.1)

x(t)=t^{dn},\quad y(t)=t^{dm}+\text{ h.o.t. },\quad m>n,\penalty\ \penalty\ \gcd(n,m)=1.

Here, ‘h.o.t.’ stands for ‘higher order terms’.

The local defining equation of
(C,0)
can be written as
f(x,y)=0
, with (see
[
15
, Prop. I.3.4]
)

f(x,y):=\prod_{j=0}^{dn-1}(y-y(\zeta_{dn}^{j}x^{\frac{1}{dn}}))\in\mathbf{k}\{x\}[y],\quad\zeta_{dn}:=e^{\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{dn}},

which is monic and irreducible in
y
of degree
dn
.
The relevant reductive group will be
G=GL_{dn}
.

Let
R=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{C,0}=\mathbf{k}[[x(t),y(t)]]
be the completed local ring, with normalization
\tilde{R}=\mathbf{k}[[t]]
and quotient field
E=\mathbf{k}((t))
.
Set
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{F}=\mathbf{k}[[x=t^{dn}]]
with fraction field
F=\mathbf{k}((x))
.
Then
R
is a free
\mathcal{O}
-module of rank
dn
:

R=\oplus_{j=0}^{dn-1}\mathcal{O}\cdot y^{j}.

Let
\gamma\in\mathfrak{g}(F)
, with
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{dn}
, denotes the matrix of multiplication by
y
relative to the basis
(1,y,\dots,y^{dn-1})
. Explicitly,

(2.2)

\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&\ldots&0&a_{0}\\
1&\ddots&\vdots&a_{1}\\
&\ddots&0&\vdots\\
&&1&a_{dn-1}\end{array}\right),\quad f(x,y)=y^{dn}-a_{dn-1}y^{dn-1}-\cdots-a_{0},\quad a_{k}(x)\in x\mathbf{k}[[x]].

Definition 2.1

(
[
13
, Thm. 1.1]
)
.

The planar curve singularity
(C,0)
is called
generic
2
2

2

See Remark
2.7
for a justification.
if it admits a parametrization:

x(t)=t^{dn},\quad y(t)=t^{dm}+\lambda t^{dm+1}+\text{ h.o.t. },\quad\lambda\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}.

2.2.
Hilbert schemes of points

We briefly recall the notion of generalized affine Springer fibers (GASF).
As above, let
\mathcal{O}=\mathbf{k}[[x]]
and
F=\mathbf{k}((t))
.
For a reductive group
G
over
\mathbf{k}
and a finite-dimensional representation
V
of
G
, the affine Grassmannian is
\mathbf{Gr}_{G}:=G(F)/G(\mathcal{O})
.

Definition 2.2
.

For
\mathfrak{v}\in V(F)
, the
generalized affine Springer fiber (GASF)
associated with
(\mathfrak{v},G,V)
is the closed sub-ind-scheme of
\mathbf{Gr}_{G}
defined by

\mathrm{Spr}_{\mathfrak{v}}(A):=\{g\in G(A((t)))\mid g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{v}\in V(A[[t]])\}/G(A[[t]]),\quad A\in\mathbf{k}\text{-alg}.

When
V=\mathfrak{g}
is the adjoint representation,
\mathrm{Spr}_{\mathfrak{v}}
reduces to the usual affine Springer fiber (ASF).

Definition 2.3
.

The (punctual) Hilbert scheme of
\tau
points on
(C,0)
is the moduli space

\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)(A):=\{\text{ideals of colength $\tau$ in }A[[x,y]]/(f)\},\quad A\in\mathbf{k}\text{-alg}.

The full Hilbert scheme is

\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\bullet]}(C,0):=\sqcup_{\tau\geq 0}\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0).

Lemma 2.4

(
[
10
, Thm. 3.3]
)
.

Let
G=GL_{dn}
and
V:=\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathbf{k}^{dn}
, the direct sum of the adjoint and standard representations.
Set
\mathfrak{v}:=(\gamma^{\top},e_{dn})
, with
e_{dn}=(0,\dots,0,1)^{\top}\in\mathbf{k}^{dn}
. Then

\mathrm{Spr}_{\mathfrak{v}}\cong\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\bullet]}(C,0).

This explains the terminology in Theorem
1.2
, except for the affine paving.

From now on
, by abuse of notation, we identify a
\mathbf{k}
-scheme
X
with its set of
\mathbf{k}
-points
X(\mathbf{k})
.

2.3.
Semigroup and semimodules of valuations

Recall that

\delta=\delta(R):=\dim\tilde{R}/R<\infty\quad\text{({$\delta$-invariant})}.

Since
R
is Gorenstein,

c(R):=\min\{c\in\mathbb{Z}\mid t^{c}\tilde{R}\subset R\}=2\delta\quad\text{({conductor})}.

Let
\mathrm{ord}_{t}:E\to\mathbb{Z}\sqcup\{\infty\}
be the
t
-adic valuation.

Definition 2.5
.

For a nonzero finitely generated
R
-submodule
J\subset E
, set

\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J):=\{\mathrm{ord}_{t}(z):z\in J\setminus\{0\}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}.

In particular,

(2.3)

\Gamma:=\mathrm{ord}_{t}(R)\subset\mathbb{N}

is a
semigroup
of valuations, and
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)
is a
\Gamma
-
semimodule
3
3

3

Here a
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subset\mathbb{Z}
means:
a\in\Gamma
,
b\in\Delta

\Rightarrow

a+b\in\Delta
.
.

For any nonempty
\Gamma
-semimodule
S\subset\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\min S\in\mathbb{Z}

\Leftrightarrow

S\neq\mathbb{Z}
, since
s\in S
implies

s+2\delta+\mathbb{N}\subset s+\Gamma\subset S.

In particular, this is the case for
J\subset E
as above:
Say,
J=Rz_{1}+\cdots+Rz_{r}
with
z_{i}\in E^{\times}
. Then

\mathrm{ord}_{t}(z)\geq\min_{i}\mathrm{ord}_{t}(z_{i})>-\infty,

for any
z=\sum_{i}a_{i}z_{i}\in J
,
a_{i}\in R
.

Definition 2.6
.

For a nonempty
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subsetneq\mathbb{Z}
, define the
conductor

c(\Delta):=\min\{c\in\mathbb{Z}\mid c+\mathbb{N}\subset\Delta\}.

Clearly
\min\Delta\leq c(\Delta)\leq\min\Delta+2\delta
.
If
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
, define the
\delta
-invariant

\delta(\Delta):=|\mathbb{N}\setminus\Delta|.

Finally,
\Delta
is called
0
-normalized
if
\min\Delta=0
.

By definition, for a nonzero finitely generated
R
-submodule
J\subset E
, we have

c(\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J))=c(J):=\min\{c\in\mathbb{Z}\mid t^{c}\tilde{R}\subset J\}.

If
J\subset\tilde{R}
, then
\delta(\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J))=\delta(J):=\dim\tilde{R}/J
.
In particular
\delta(\Gamma)=\delta
and
c(\Gamma)=c(R)=2\delta
.

Remark 2.7
.

In the parametrization (
2.1
) for
(C,0)
, by a further formal coordinate change
(x,y)\mapsto(x,y+h(x,y))
with
h\in\langle x,y\rangle^{2}\subset\mathbf{k}[[x,y]]
, we may assume

y(t)=t^{dm}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 1\\
dm+j\notin\Gamma\end{subarray}}\lambda_{j}t^{dm+j},\quad\lambda_{j}\in\mathbf{k}.

In particular,
\lambda_{j}\neq 0\Rightarrow dm+j\leq 2\delta-1
. This does not affect
R
.

For fixed
\delta
, among parametrizations

x(t)=t^{dn},\quad y(t)=t^{dm}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq 2\delta-1}\lambda_{j}t^{dm+j},

the
generic
singularities (those with
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\neq 0
) form a Zariski open dense subset.
This justifies Definition
2.1
.

Lemma 2.8
.

For any nonempty
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
:

c(\Delta)\leq\delta+\delta(\Delta).

If
\Delta
is
0
-normalized then

\delta(\Delta)\leq\delta,

with equality
\delta(\Delta)=\delta
iff
\Delta=\Gamma
iff
c(\Delta)=\delta+\delta(\Delta)
.

Proof.

Since
c(\Delta)-1\notin\Delta
and
\Delta
is a
\Gamma
-semimodule, we have

a\in[0,c(\Delta)-1]\cap\Gamma\ \Rightarrow\ c(\Delta)-1-a\notin\Delta.

Hence

\delta(\Delta)=|[0,c(\Delta)-1]\setminus\Delta|\geq|[0,c(\Delta)-1]\cap\Gamma|\geq c(\Delta)-\delta,

which gives
c(\Delta)\leq\delta+\delta(\Delta)
.

If
\Delta
is
0
-normalized, then
\Gamma\subset\Delta
and
\delta(\Delta)\leq|\mathbb{N}\setminus\Gamma|=\delta
. Equality holds iff
\Delta=\Gamma
.
Finally, the condition
c(\Delta)=\delta+\delta(\Delta)
forces
c(\Delta)=2\delta
and
\delta(\Delta)=\delta
, hence
\Delta=\Gamma
. The converse is immediate.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

For any
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
, set

e(\Delta):=\delta-\delta(\Delta)\in[0,\delta],\qquad\overline{\Delta}:=e(\Delta)+\Delta\subset e(\Delta)+\mathbb{N}.

In particular,
\min\overline{\Delta}=e(\Delta)
, and

(2.4)

\delta(\overline{\Delta})=|\mathbb{N}-\overline{\Delta}|=e(\Delta)+|(e(\Delta)+\mathbb{N})-(e(\Delta)+\Delta)|=e(\Delta)+\delta(\Delta)=\delta;\quad c(\overline{\Delta})=c(\Delta)+e(\Delta)\leq 2\delta.

Conversely, any
\Gamma
-semimodule
\overline{\Delta}\subset\mathbb{N}
with
\delta(\overline{\Delta})=\delta
arises as
\overline{\Delta}=e(\Delta)+\Delta
for a unique
0
-normalized
\Delta
, with
e(\Delta)=\min\overline{\Delta}
.

Definition 2.10
.

For any bounded below
dn
-invariant subset
\Delta\subset\mathbb{Z}
, define
A(\Delta)
as the set of
dn
-generators
of
\Delta
. That is,

\Delta=\sqcup_{a\in A(\Delta)}a+dn\mathbb{N}.

2.4.
(Local) compactified Jacobians

Let
0\neq J\subset E
be a finitely generated
R
-submodule. We record two basic properties:

(i)

J
is a
torsion-free coherent
R
-module of (generic) rank
1
.

Indeed, set
S:=R\setminus\{0\}
so that
E=S^{-1}R
. Since
0\neq J\subset E
, we have

0\neq J\subset S^{-1}J\subset E.

But
S^{-1}J
is a nonzero
E
-vector space, hence equal to
E
.
Done.

(ii)

J
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-module of rank
dn
.

Write
J=t^{-N}J_{0}
for some
N\geq 0
and
J_{0}\subset\tilde{R}
a nonzero
R
-submodule.
Since
\mathcal{O}_{F}
is a PID,
J_{0}
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-module of finite rank. Moreover,

\delta(J_{0})=\delta(\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J_{0}))=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\tilde{R}/J_{0}=|\mathbb{N}-\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J_{0})|\leq c(J_{0})<\infty,

so
\tilde{R}/J_{0}
is a finitely generated torsion
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-module.
Tensoring with
F
gives
F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\tilde{R}/J_{0}=0
.
Applying the exact functor
F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}-
to

0\to J_{0}\to\tilde{R}\to\tilde{R}/J_{0}\to 0

yields
F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}J_{0}\cong F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\tilde{R}=\oplus_{j=0}^{dn-1}F\cdot t^{j}
.
Hence
J_{0}
, and therefore
J
, is a free
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-module of rank
dn
.

Definition 2.11
.

The
(local) compactified Jacobian
of
(C,0)
is

\overline{J}(C,0):=\{J\subset E\mid J\text{ an $R$-submodule with }J\subset t^{-N}\tilde{R},\penalty\ \dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/J=\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/R,\penalty\ N\gg 0\}.

The terminology is explained as follows:

•

For
J\in\overline{J}(C,0)
and
J\subset t^{-N}\tilde{R}
, the Noetherian property ensures that
J
is finitely generated over
R
.

•

By (i) above,
J
can be viewed as a torsion-free rank
1
coherent sheaf on
\mathrm{Spec}R
, of
degree
0
, where

\deg(J):=\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/J-\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/R=0,

independently of
N\gg 0
(see Lemma
2.13
).

Lemma 2.12
.

For
J\in\overline{J}(C,0)
,

t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\subset J\subset\tilde{R},\qquad\dim\tilde{R}/J=\delta.

Equivalently,

\overline{J}(C,0)=\{J\subset\tilde{R}\mid J\text{ an $R$-submodule with }\dim\tilde{R}/J=\delta\}.

Proof.

Let
v_{m}:=\min\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)
. Choose
z\in J
with
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(z)=v_{m}
and set
J_{0}:=z^{-1}J
.
Then
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J_{0})=\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)-v_{m}
is a
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodule, so
J_{0}\subset\tilde{R}
.
By Lemma
2.8
,
\delta(J_{0})\leq\delta
.

Suppose
J\subset t^{-N}\tilde{R}
with
N\gg 0
. Then

\dim(t^{-N}\tilde{R}/J)=|(-N+\mathbb{N})\setminus(v_{m}+\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J_{0}))|=|(-N-v_{m}+\mathbb{N})\setminus\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J_{0})|=N+v_{m}+\delta(J_{0})\leq N+v_{m}+\delta.

But by assumption this dimension equals
\dim(t^{-N}\tilde{R}/R)=N+\delta
, hence
v_{m}\geq 0
. Thus
J\subset\tilde{R}
.

Now,
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)\subset\mathbb{N}
is a nonzero
\Gamma
-semimodule with
\delta(\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J))=\delta(J)=\delta
. By Lemma
2.8
,
c(J)\leq 2\delta
, i.e.
t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\subset J
.
∎

Recall that
E=K(R)=\oplus_{j=0}^{dn-1}Fy^{j}
.
For
G=GL_{dn}
, the map

[g]=gG(\mathcal{O})\mapsto(1,y,\cdots,y^{dn-1})g\mathcal{O}^{dn}

induces a natural identification

\mathbf{Gr}_{G}\cong\{\Lambda:\Lambda\text{ is an }\mathcal{O}\text{-lattice in $E$, i.e., finitely generated (projective) $\mathcal{O}$-submodule s.t. $F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\Lambda=E$}\}.

Moreover, the determinant
\det:G=GL_{dn}\rightarrow GL_{1}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
induces

(2.5)

\det:\mathbf{Gr}_{G}\rightarrow\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}=F^{\times}/\mathcal{O}^{\times}\cong\mathbb{Z}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\text{non-reduced}):[g]\mapsto[\det g]\cong\mathrm{ord}_{x}\det(g).

For clarity,
set

(2.6)

\deg([g]):=\mathrm{ord}_{x}\det(g)=\frac{1}{dn}\mathrm{ord}_{t}\det(g)\in\mathbb{Z}.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
[g]\in\mathbf{Gr}_{G}
with associated lattice
\Lambda=(1,y,\dots,y^{dn-1})g\mathcal{O}^{dn}
. Then

\deg(\Lambda):=\deg([g])=\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/\Lambda-\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/R,\qquad N\gg 0.

Moreover,
\deg(t\Lambda)=\deg(\Lambda)+1
.

Proof.

Choose
N\gg 0
such that
\Lambda,R\subset t^{-N}\tilde{R}
.
Write
t^{-N}\tilde{R}=\oplus_{i=0}^{dn-1}\mathcal{O}\cdot z_{i}
and
(1,y,\cdots,y^{dn-1})=(z_{0},z_{1},\cdots,z_{dn-1})h
,
h\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathcal{O})
.
Then
(1,y,\cdots,y^{dn-1})g=(z_{0},z_{1},\cdots,z_{dn-1})hg
is an
\mathcal{O}
-basis of
\Lambda
.
As
\mathcal{O}
is a PID, by the structure theorem for finitely generated
\mathcal{O}
-modules, we have

\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/R=\mathrm{ord}_{x}\det(h);\quad\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/\Lambda=\mathrm{ord}_{x}\det(hg)=\mathrm{ord}_{x}\det(h)+\mathrm{ord}_{x}\det(g).

The first claim follows.

For the second, note
t\Lambda\subset t^{1-N}\tilde{R}\subset t^{-N}\tilde{R}
, and
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(t\Lambda)=1+\mathrm{ord}_{t}(\Lambda)
, so

\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/t\Lambda=|(-N+\mathbb{N})\setminus\mathrm{ord}_{t}(t\Lambda)|=|(-N-1+\mathbb{N})\setminus\mathrm{ord}_{t}\Lambda|=1+|(-N+\mathbb{N})\setminus\mathrm{ord}_{t}\Lambda|=1+\dim t^{-N}\tilde{R}/\Lambda.

Combining with the first claim yields
\deg(t\Lambda)=\deg(\Lambda)+1
.
∎

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\mathrm{Spr}_{\gamma}
be the affine Springer fiber associated to
(\gamma,G=GL_{dn},\mathfrak{g})
.
Then,

(2.7)

\mathbb{Z}\times\overline{J}(C,0)\cong\mathrm{Spr}_{\gamma}:(v,J)\mapsto t^{v}J,

as (reduced) ind-varieties.

Note.
Since
\gamma
is
G(F)
-conjugate to
\gamma^{\top}
, also
\mathrm{Spr}_{\gamma}\cong\mathrm{Spr}_{\gamma^{\top}}
.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda\subset E
be an
\mathcal{O}
-lattice. Then
\Lambda
is an
R
-module iff
y\Lambda\subset\Lambda
, i.e.

g^{-1}\cdot\gamma\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathcal{O})\quad\text{for }\Lambda=(1,y,\dots,y^{dn-1})g\mathcal{O}^{dn},

which is precisely the condition
[g]\in\mathrm{Spr}_{\gamma}
.
The identification with
\mathbb{Z}\times\overline{J}(C,0)
now follows from Lemma
2.13
. In particular,

\overline{J}(C,0)=\{\Lambda\in\mathrm{Spr}_{\gamma}:\deg(\Lambda)=0\}.

∎

Remark 2.15
.

Goresky–Kottwitz–MacPherson
[
12
]
proved purity of affine Springer fibers
\mathrm{Spr}_{\mathfrak{v}}
for regular integral equivalued semisimple
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathfrak{g}(F)
.
For
G=GL_{dn}
, this means
\mathfrak{v}
is
G(\overline{F})
-conjugate to

\mathrm{diag}(a_{1}x^{s},\dots,a_{dn}x^{s})+\text{h.o.t.},

with
s\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
,
a_{i}\in\mathbf{k}
distinct.
Consequently, our
\gamma\in G(F)
is regular integral equivalued semisimple iff
d=1
, i.e.
(C,0)
is quasi-homogeneous:
x=t^{n}
,
y=t^{m}
,
\gcd(n,m)=1
.

2.5.
The (local) Abel–Jacobi map

For
\tau\geq 0
, recall that the Hilbert scheme of
\tau
points on
(C,0)
is

\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0):=\{I\subset R\penalty\ |\penalty\ I\text{ is an ideal with }\dim R/I=\tau\}.

In particular,
\mathrm{Hilb}^{[0]}(C,0)=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
.

Lemma 2.16

(
[
31
, Thm. 3]
)
.

There is a closed embedding

\phi_{\tau}:\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)\hookrightarrow\overline{J}(C,0),\quad I\mapsto t^{-\tau}I.

Moreover, for
J\in\overline{J}(C,0)
, one has
I:=t^{\tau}J\in\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)

iff
t^{\tau}J\subset R
. In particular,
\phi_{\tau}
is an isomorphism for
\tau\geq 2\delta
.

Definition 2.17
.

For a nonzero finitely generated
R
-submodule
J\subset E
, define the
dual
R
-module

(2.8)

J^{-1}:=\{z\in E:\penalty\ zJ\subset R\}\subset E.

It is immediate that
J^{-1}
is again a finitely generated
R
-module.
Some basic properties are:

(a)

As a
R
-module,
J^{-1}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(J,R):z\mapsto z
.
The inverse
\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(J,R)\xrightarrow{\cong}J^{-1}:h\mapsto r_{h}
is defined as follows:
\forall h\in\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(I,R)
, we get a commutative diagram of
R
-modules

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.701pt}{-12.29362pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{73}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Take any nonzero
z\in J\subset E
, then by
E
-linearity,
(S^{-1}h)(az)=ah(z)=az\cdot(z^{-1}h(z))
,
\forall a\in E
.
Moreover, for any other nonzero
w\in J\subset E
, we have
w=az
for some
a\in E^{\times}
. It follows that
w^{-1}h(w)=z^{-1}a^{-1}ah(z)=z^{-1}h(z)
.
So,
r_{h}:=z^{-1}h(z)
is independent of
z\in J\setminus\{0\}
, and
r_{h}w=h(w)\in R
,
\forall w\in J\setminus\{0\}
. In other words,
r_{h}\in J^{-1}
.

(b)

R^{-1}=R
.

(c)

Since
R
is Gorenstein,
(J^{-1})^{-1}=J

[
18
, Lem. 1.1]
.

(d)

If
J_{1}\subset J_{2}
, then
J_{2}^{-1}\subset J_{1}^{-1}
.

Lemma 2.18
.

For any
J\in\overline{J}(C,0)
:

(1)

t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\subset J^{-1}\subset\tilde{R}
and
\dim\tilde{R}/J^{-1}=\delta
.
In particular, we get an involution

\iota:\overline{J}(C,0)\xrightarrow{\;\cong\;}\overline{J}(C,0):J\mapsto J^{-1}.

(2)

For
a\in[0,2\delta-1]
,

a\in\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;2\delta-1-a\notin\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J^{-1}).

In particular,

\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J^{-1})=[0,2\delta-1]\setminus(2\delta-1-\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J))\;\sqcup\;[2\delta,\infty).

Proof.

(1)
. We first claim that
\tilde{R}^{-1}=t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}
. Indeed
z\in\tilde{R}^{-1}
iff
z\tilde{R}\subset R
, forcing
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(z)\geq 2\delta
. Conversely,
t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\subset R
.

Now by Lemma
2.12
, we have
t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\subset J\subset\tilde{R}
.
Taking duals gives

\tilde{R}^{-1}=t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\subset J^{-1}\subset(t^{2\delta}\tilde{R})^{-1}=\tilde{R}.

To compute
\dim\tilde{R}/J^{-1}
, apply
\mathrm{RHom}_{R}(-,R)
to the short exact sequence

0\to J\to\tilde{R}\to\tilde{R}/J\to 0.

Since
R
is Gorenstein of dimension
1
, we have
[
17
, Prop. 4.9, Prop. 4.13]
:

(A)

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{R}(-,R)
is a duality on finite-length
R
-modules, preserving length.

(B)

\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{R}(M,R)=0
for
i\neq 1
if
M
has finite length.

(C)

\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{R}(M,R)=0
for all
i>0
if
M
is finitely generated torsion-free
[
18
, Lem. 1.1]
.

(D)

If
R
is a local
\mathbf{k}
-algebra with residue field
\mathbf{k}
, then
\ell_{R}(M)=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}M
.

Using these, we obtain an exact sequence

\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\tilde{R}/J,R)=0\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\tilde{R},R)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(J,R)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{R}^{1}(\tilde{R}/J,R)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{R}^{1}(\tilde{R},R)=0,

where
\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\tilde{R},R)=\tilde{R}^{-1}=t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(J,R)=J^{-1}
.
Hence

J^{-1}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\;\cong\;\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{R}(\tilde{R}/J,R).

By (A), the latter has length
\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\tilde{R}/J=\delta
.
Thus
\dim\tilde{R}/J^{-1}=2\delta-\dim J^{-1}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}=\delta
.

(2)
By (1),
t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\subset J^{-1}\subset\tilde{R}
.
For
a\in[0,2\delta-1]
, observe that if
z\in J
and
w\in J^{-1}
then
zw\in R
, so

\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)+\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J^{-1})\subset\Gamma.

Since
2\delta-1\notin\Gamma
, we deduce

a\in\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)\;\Rightarrow\;2\delta-1-a\notin\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J^{-1}).

But both sides contain exactly
\delta
elements in
[0,2\delta-1]
, so the implication is an equivalence.
The alternative description of
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J^{-1})
follows.
∎

Definition 2.19
.

The composition

(2.9)

\mathrm{AJ}_{\tau}:=\iota\circ\phi_{\tau}:\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)\hookrightarrow\overline{J}(C,0),\quad I\mapsto t^{\tau}I^{-1},

is called the (
\tau
-th)
local Abel–Jacobi map
.

Corollary 2.20
.

For
J\in\overline{J}(C,0)
,

(2.10)

J\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{AJ}_{\tau})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad t^{\tau}\in J.

In particular,
t^{\tau}\in J
automatically if
\tau\geq c(J)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.16
and property (d) above,

J\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{AJ}_{\tau})\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;J^{-1}\in\mathrm{Im}(\phi_{\tau})\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;t^{\tau}J^{-1}\subset R\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;t^{\tau}\in J.

The last claim is immediate since
c(J)
is the conductor of
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(J)
.
∎

3.
Affine paving and purity

Let
(C,0)
be a
generic
irreducible planar curve singularity as in Definition
2.1
.
The smooth case is trivial, so we
assume

(C,0)
is singular.
By Remark
2.7
, we may take
(C,0)
to be parametrized by

(3.1)

x(t)=t^{dn},\quad y(t)=t^{dm}+\lambda t^{dm+1}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 2\\
dm+j\notin\Gamma\end{subarray}}\lambda_{j}t^{dm+j},\quad(n,m)=1,\;m>n>1,\;\lambda\in\mathbf{k}^{\times},\;\lambda_{j}\in\mathbf{k}.

Thus
(C,0)
has two
Puiseux pairs
,
(n,m)
and
(d,dm+1)
, and its
singularity knot

K_{(C,0)}
is the
(d,dmn+1)
-cable of the
(n,m)
-torus knot.
The semigroup is generated by
dn,dm,dmn+1
, i.e.

(3.2)

\Gamma=\bigsqcup_{0\leq i\leq d-1,\;0\leq j\leq n-1}\bigl(\hat{a}_{i,j}+dn\mathbb{N}\bigr),\quad\hat{a}_{i,j}:=jdm+(dmn+1)i.

Recall that a
\mathbf{k}
-variety
X
admits an
affine paving
if there exists an increasing finite closed filtration

\emptyset=X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset\cdots\subset X_{r}=X

such that each difference
X_{i+1}\setminus X_{i}
is isomorphic to an affine space
\mathbb{A}^{k}
.

3.1.
The affine paving for the local compactified Jacobian

We now recall Gorsky–Mazin–Oblomkov’s affine paving for the local compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}(C,0)
associated to
(C,0)
.

3.1.1.
Cell decomposition via semimodules of valuations

Definition 3.1
.

A
standard module
for
(C,0)
is an
R
-submodule
M\subset\tilde{R}
with
\min\mathrm{ord}_{t}(M)=0
.
In particular,
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(M)\subset\mathbb{N}
is a
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodule.

Definition 3.2
.

For a
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
, define

J_{\Delta}:=\{\,M\subset\tilde{R}\;\mid\;M\text{ is a standard $R$-submodule with }\mathrm{ord}_{t}(M)=\Delta\,\}.\quad(\text{possibly empty})

Lemma 3.3
.

There is a canonical locally closed embedding

\iota_{\Delta}:J_{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\overline{J}(C,0),\quad M\mapsto t^{e(\Delta)}M.

Moreover,

\overline{J}(C,0)=\bigsqcup_{\Delta}\iota_{\Delta}(J_{\Delta}).

Proof.

Immediate from Lemma
2.12
and Definition
2.9
.
∎

3.1.2.
Alternative description via Schubert cells

By Lemma
2.12
, there is a natural identification (as reduced schemes):

\overline{J}(C,0)\cong\{\Lambda\in\mathbf{Gr}(\delta,\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}):x\Lambda\subset\Lambda,\,y\Lambda\subset\Lambda\}\subset\mathbf{Gr}(\delta,\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}),

where
\mathbf{Gr}(\delta,\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R})
is the Grassmannian of
\delta
-dimensional subspaces of

\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\cong\bigoplus_{j=0}^{2\delta-1}\mathbf{k}\cdot t^{j}
.

Define the canonical flag

0=V_{0}\subset V_{1}\subset\cdots\subset V_{2\delta}=\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R},\quad V_{i}:=t^{2\delta-i}\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}\cong\oplus_{j=2\delta-i}^{2\delta-1}\mathbf{k}\cdot t^{j}.

For integers
\delta\geq a_{1}\geq\cdots\geq a_{\delta}\geq 0
, the
Schubert cell

(3.3)

W_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{\delta}}:=\{\Lambda\in\mathbf{Gr}(\delta,\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}):\penalty\ \dim(\Lambda\cap V_{j})=i\text{ for }\delta+i-a_{i}\leq j<\delta+i+1-a_{i+1}\}m

gives the Schubert cell decomposition
[
31
, p.106]

\mathbf{Gr}(\delta,\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R})=\sqcup_{\delta\geq a_{1}\geq\cdots\geq a_{\delta}\geq 0}W_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{\delta}},\quad W_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{\delta}}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\delta^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{\delta}a_{i}}.

Moreover,
W_{b_{1},\dots,b_{\delta}}\subset\overline{W}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{\delta}}
iff
b_{i}\geq a_{i}
for all
i
, which defines an affine paving of the Grassmannian.

In valuation terms:

W_{a_{1},\dots,a_{\delta}}=\{\Lambda\in\mathbf{Gr}(\delta,\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R}):\mathrm{ord}_{t}(\Lambda)\cap[0,2\delta-1]=\{\delta+a_{i}-i:1\leq i\leq\delta\}\}.

Hence, for any
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
,

\iota_{\Delta}(J_{\Delta})\cong\overline{J}(C,0)\cap W_{a_{1},\dots,a_{\delta}},

with
a_{i}
determined by

\{\delta+a_{1}-1>\cdots>\delta+a_{\delta}-\delta\}=\overline{\Delta}\cap[0,2\delta-1],\quad\overline{\Delta}:=e(\Delta)+\Delta.

3.1.3.
Admissibility and affine paving

It remains to determine
J_{\Delta}
, in particular, when
J_{\Delta}
is nonempty.

Definition 3.4
.

Fix any
(dn,dm)
-invariant subset
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

For
\ell\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq d-1
, say
i
is
\ell
-admissible

4
4

4

In
[
13
, Def.3.7]
, this corresponds to
\ell
not being
i
-suspicious.

if there exists
a\in A(\Delta)
with
a\equiv i\pmod{d}
and
a+dm+\ell\in\Delta
.
We write “admissible” for
\ell=1
.

(2)

\Delta
is
admissible
if every
i\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
is admissible.

Finally,
denote
by
\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
the set of all admissible
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodules.

For a more concrete description of
\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
, see Section
3.3
.

Definition 3.5
.

For
\Delta\in\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
and
a\in\Delta
, set

\mathrm{Gaps}(a)=\mathrm{Gaps}_{\Delta}(a):=[a,\infty)\setminus\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}.

Define

\dim\Delta:=\sum_{a\in A(\Delta)}|\mathrm{Gaps}(a)|-\sum_{a\in A(\Delta)}|\mathrm{Gaps}(a+dm)|.

Lemma 3.6

(
[
13
, Prop.3.23]
)
.

For any
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
,

J_{\Delta}\neq\emptyset\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Delta\in\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm).

In this case,

J_{\Delta}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\dim\Delta}.

Corollary 3.7
.

For a generic irreducible planar curve singularity
(C,0)
, the compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}(C,0)
admits an affine paving

\overline{J}(C,0)=\bigsqcup_{\Delta\in\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)}\iota_{\Delta}(J_{\Delta}),

with the filtration induced from the Schubert cell paving of
\mathbf{Gr}(\delta,\tilde{R}/t^{2\delta}\tilde{R})
.

3.2.
The affine paving for the Hilbert scheme of points

We now arrive at our main goal: the affine paving of the Hilbert scheme

\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)
, when
(C,0)
is a
generic
irreducible planar curve singularity.

Definition 3.8
.

For any
0
-normalized
\Gamma
-semimodule
\Delta\subset\mathbb{N}
, define

(3.4)

\displaystyle H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\,I\in\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)\;\big|\;\mathrm{AJ}_{\tau}(I)\in\iota_{\Delta}(J_{\Delta}),\text{ i.e. }\mathrm{ord}_{t}(t^{\tau-e(\Delta)}I^{-1})=\Delta\,\}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\{\,M\in J_{\Delta}\;\big|\;t^{\tau-e(\Delta)}\in M\,\}.

The second description follows from Corollary
2.20
with

J=\mathrm{AJ}_{\tau}(I)=t^{e(\Delta)}M
.
Equivalently, via the Abel–Jacobian map
\mathrm{AJ}_{\tau}:\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)\hookrightarrow\overline{J}(C,0)
,

H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}=\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)\cap\iota_{\Delta}(J_{\Delta}).

This gives a decomposition into locally closed subvarieties:

(3.5)

\mathrm{Hilb}^{[\tau]}(C,0)=\bigsqcup_{\Delta}H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}.

Remark 3.9
.

For any
M\in J_{\Delta}
, the condition
t^{\tau-e(\Delta)}\in M
is automatic whenever

\tau-e(\Delta)\geq c(M)=c(\Delta)
. Thus,

(3.6)

H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}\cong J_{\Delta},\quad\forall\tau\geq c(\Delta)+e(\Delta)=c(\overline{\Delta}).

Theorem 3.10
.

We have
H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}\neq\emptyset
if and only if

\Delta\in\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
and
\tau_{0}:=\tau-e(\Delta)\in\Delta
.
In this case,

(3.7)

H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\dim\Delta-|\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{0})|}.

Proof.

The “only if” part is clear.
It remains to show that for any
\Delta\in\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
with
\tau_{0}=\tau-e(\Delta)\in\Delta
,

H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\dim\Delta-|\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{0})|}.

See Example
3.12
for an illustration.

Step 1. Preparations.
Fix
\Delta\in\mathcal{A}dm(dn,dm)
.
Let
A(\Delta)=\{\hat{b}_{i,j}\mid 0\leq i\leq d-1,\,0\leq j\leq n-1\}
be the set of
dn
-generators of
\Delta

with
\hat{b}_{i,j}\equiv jdm+i\pmod{dn}
.
For any standard
R
-module
M\subset\tilde{R}
with
\mathrm{ord}_{t}(M)=\Delta
, we may write uniquely

(3.8)

M=\bigoplus_{a\in A(\Delta)}\mathcal{O}_{F}\cdot g_{a}=\bigoplus_{0\leq i\leq d-1,\,0\leq j\leq n-1}\mathcal{O}_{F}\cdot\beta_{i,j},\quad\beta_{i,j}=g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}},

where for each
a\in\Delta
there exists a unique element

(3.9)

g_{a}=t^{a}+\sum_{\ell\in[a,\infty)\setminus\Delta}g_{a;\ell-a}t^{\ell},\qquad g_{a;\ell-a}\in\mathbf{k}.

For convenience, set

g_{a;0}:=1,\qquad g_{a;\ell-a}:=0\quad\text{for all }\ell\in(a,\infty)\cap\Delta,\;a\in\Delta.

Conversely, given elements
g_{a}
for
a\in A(\Delta)
as above, define

M:=\bigoplus_{a\in A(\Delta)}\mathcal{O}_{F}\cdot g_{a}.

Then
M
is a standard
R
-module if and only if
yM\subset M
.
Concretely, for each
a\in A(\Delta)
, viewing the coefficients
g_{a;j-a}
with
j\in\mathrm{Gaps}(a)
as
variables
, there exists a unique element

(3.10)

s_{a+dm}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathrm{Gaps}(a+dm)}s_{a+dm;\,\ell-a-dm}\,t^{\ell}

such that:

•

each
s_{a+dm;\,\ell-a-dm}
is a polynomial in the variables
g_{a^{\prime};\ell^{\prime}}
,
a^{\prime}\in A(\Delta),\ell^{\prime}>0
;

•

yg_{a}-s_{a+dm}\in\oplus_{a\in A(\Delta)}\mathcal{O}_{F}g_{a}
.

Then,

(3.11)

M\in J_{\Delta}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;yM\subset M\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;s_{a+dm;\,\ell-a-dm}=0,\quad\forall\,\ell\in\mathrm{Gaps}(a+dm),\;a\in A(\Delta).

In this case, by Corollary
2.20
,

\iota_{\Delta}(M)\in H_{\Delta}^{[\tau]}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad g_{\tau_{0}}=t^{\tau_{0}}.

To prepare for explicit calculations, we now determine the formulas for
s_{a+dm}
(
a\in A(\Delta)
) and for
g_{b}
(
b\in\Delta
).
Since

\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm\in\Delta,\qquad\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm\equiv\hat{b}_{i,j+1}\pmod{dn},

for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/n
, we may write

\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm=\hat{b}_{i,j+1}+dn\alpha_{i,j},\qquad\alpha_{i,j}\geq 0.

For
\ell\geq 1
, set

(3.12)

g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}:=g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell}-g_{\hat{b}_{i,j+1};\ell},\qquad R_{i,j;\ell}:=\lambda_{2}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell-2}+\cdots+\lambda_{\ell}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};0},

with the convention
R_{i,j;1}=0
.
Then,

(3.13)

yg_{\hat{b}_{i,j}}-x^{\alpha_{i,j}}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j+1}}=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}F_{i,j;\ell}t^{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell},\quad F_{i,j;\ell}:=g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}+\lambda g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell-1}+R_{i,j;\ell}.

It follows that

\displaystyle s_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\geq 1}F_{i,j;\ell}t^{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell}-\sum_{\ell_{0}\geq 1:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell_{0}\in\Delta}F_{i,j;\ell_{0}}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\geq 1:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell\notin\Delta}(F_{i,j;\ell}-\sum_{1\leq\ell_{0}<\ell:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell_{0}\in\Delta}F_{i,j;\ell_{0}}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell_{0};\ell-\ell_{0}})t^{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell}.

Hence,

(3.14)

s_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm;\ell}=F_{i,j;\ell}-\sum_{1\leq\ell_{0}<\ell:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell_{0}\in\Delta}F_{i,j;\ell_{0}}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell_{0};\ell-\ell_{0}}.

Finally, the coefficients
g_{b;\ell}
(
b\in\Delta
,
b+\ell\in\mathrm{Gaps}(b)
) are polynomials in the basic variables

g_{a;j}
(
a\in A(\Delta)
,
a+j\in\mathrm{Gaps}(a)
), determined inductively:

•

If
b\geq c(\Delta)
, then
g_{b}=t^{b}
and hence
g_{b;\ell}=0
.

•

Otherwise, suppose
g_{b^{\prime}}
is known for all
b^{\prime}>b
in
\Delta
.
Write
b=a+\alpha dn
with
a\in A(\Delta)
,
\alpha\geq 0
.
Since
b=\mathrm{ord}_{t}(x^{\alpha}g_{a})
, we have

\displaystyle x^{\alpha}g_{a}=t^{b}+\sum_{j\geq 1:\penalty\ a+j\notin\Delta}g_{a;j}t^{b+j};\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ g_{a+\alpha dn}=x^{\alpha}g_{a}-\sum_{j\geq 1:\penalty\ a+j\notin\Delta,a+\alpha dn+j\in\Delta}g_{a;j}g_{a+\alpha dn+j};\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow

(3.15)

\displaystyle g_{a+\alpha dn;\ell}=g_{a;\ell}-\sum_{1\leq j<\ell:\penalty\ a+j\notin\Delta,a+\alpha dn+j\in\Delta}g_{a;j}g_{a+\alpha dn+j;\ell-j},\penalty\ \penalty\ (a+\alpha dn+\ell\notin\Delta).

By induction, this determines all
g_{b}
.

Step 2.

We recall some details from the proof of Lemma
3.6
, cf.
[
13
, Prop. 3.23]
.

Define

\deg_{+}g_{a;\ell}:=\ell\quad(a\in A(\Delta)),\qquad\deg_{+}(\lambda_{\ell}):=\ell.

It follows that
g_{b;\ell}
(
b\in\Delta
) and
s_{a+dm;\ell}
are homogeneous of degree
\deg_{+}=\ell
.
Set

(3.16)

\mathrm{Gen}_{\ell}:=\oplus_{a\in A(\Delta):\penalty\ a+\ell\in\mathrm{Gaps}(a)}\mathbf{k}\cdot g_{a;\ell}.

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}/d
,
\ell\geq 1
,
define

(3.17)

\hat{I}(i;\ell):=\{j\in\mathbb{Z}/n:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+\ell\notin\Delta\};\quad N_{i;\ell}:=|\hat{I}(i;\ell)|;\quad\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}:=\oplus_{j\in\hat{I}(i;\ell)}\mathbf{k}\cdot g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell}.

Then
\mathrm{Gen}_{\ell}=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/d}\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}
.
If
\hat{I}(i;\ell)\neq\emptyset
,
set

(3.18)

g_{i;\ell}^{+}:=\sum_{j\in\hat{I}(i;\ell)}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell};\quad\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}^{-}:=\{\sum_{j\in\hat{I}(i;\ell)}*_{\ell}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell}\in\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}:\penalty\ \sum*_{\ell}=0\}.

Thus,
\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}=\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}^{-}\oplus\mathbf{k}\cdot g_{i;\ell}^{+}
.

New basis for
\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}^{-}
.

Note that if
\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell\notin\Delta
, then also
\hat{b}_{i,j}+\ell,\hat{b}_{i,j+1}+\ell\notin\Delta
, i.e.
j,j+1\in\hat{I}(i;\ell)
.
For
i\in\mathbb{Z}/d=\{0,1,\cdots,d-1\}
,
\ell>0
:

(i)

If
i
is
\ell
-admissible (Definition
3.4
), define

(3.19)

I(i;\ell):=\{j\in\mathbb{Z}/n:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell\notin\Delta\}\subsetneq\mathbb{Z}/n.

Then
\{g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}:j\in I(i;\ell)\}
is linearly independent, and
I(i;\ell)\subsetneq\hat{I}(i;\ell)
.

(ii)

Otherwise,
\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell\notin\Delta
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/n
. So,
\hat{I}(i;\ell)=\mathbb{Z}/n
.
In this case, set

(3.20)

I(i;\ell):=\{0,\cdots,n-2\}\subsetneq\hat{I}(i;\ell).

In either case, we may complete
\{g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}:j\in I(i;\ell)\}
to a basis of
\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}^{-}
by adding elements of the form
g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell}-g_{\hat{b}_{i,k};\ell}
with
j,k\in\hat{I}(i;\ell)
.
For example, fix
j_{0}\in\hat{I}(i;\ell)\setminus I(i;\ell)
, and let
\overline{I}(i;\ell):=\hat{I}(i;\ell)\setminus(I(i;\ell)\cup\{j_{0}\})
.
Then

\mathrm{Gen}_{i;\ell}^{-}=\bigoplus_{j\in I(i;\ell)}\mathbf{k}\cdot g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}\;\oplus\;\bigoplus_{j\in\overline{I}(i;\ell)}\mathbf{k}\cdot h_{i,j;\ell}^{-},\quad h_{i,j;\ell}^{-}:=g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell}-g_{\hat{b}_{i,j_{0}};\ell}.

Next, set

\Lambda:=\mathbf{k}[\lambda^{\pm 1},\lambda_{j},j\geq 2,dm+j\notin\Gamma].

Define a
partial order
on

(3.21)

X:=\{g_{i;\ell}^{+};g_{i,j;\ell}^{-},j\in I(i;\ell);h_{i,j;\ell}^{-},j\in\overline{I}(i;\ell)\}

by

(3.22)

g_{\bullet,\bullet;\ell}^{-},h_{\bullet,\bullet;\ell}^{-}<g_{\bullet;\ell}^{+}<g_{\bullet,\bullet;\ell+1}^{-},h_{\bullet,\bullet;\ell+1}^{-};\quad g_{d-\ell;\ell}^{+}>g_{d-\ell+1;\ell}^{+}>\cdots>g_{d-\ell-1;\ell}^{+}.

Recall that

J_{\Delta}=\{s_{a+dm;\ell}=0:a\in A(\Delta),\,a+dm+\ell\notin\Delta\}.

If
i
is not
\ell
-admissible (so necessarily
\ell\geq 2
), then we replace the equation
s_{\hat{b}_{i,n-1}+dm;\ell}=0
by

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}/n}s_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm;\ell}=0.

For brevity, for
g\in X
write
\mathrm{Poly}(<g)
for any polynomial in
\Lambda[g^{\prime}\in X:g^{\prime}<g]
.
Similarly, for
k\geq 1
, write
\mathrm{Poly}(\leq k)
for any polynomial in
\Lambda[g\in X:\deg_{+}\leq k]
.
Then, by definition and induction,

(3.23)

F_{i,j;\ell}=g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}+\lambda g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell-1}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-2),\qquad g_{a+\alpha dn;\ell}=g_{a;\ell}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-1).

Now,
J_{\Delta}\subset\mathbb{A}^{G(\Delta)}=\{(g)_{g\in X}\}
is defined by

E(\Delta)=\sum_{a\in A(\Delta)}|\mathrm{Gaps}(a+dm)|
new equations as follows.
We proceed by induction on
\ell
, starting with the initial case
\ell=1
.

(1)

If
\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell\notin\Delta
such that either
j\neq n-1
or

\ell
-admissible
(Definition

), then
j\in I(i;\ell)
.
By (
3.14
), the corresponding equation is

(3.24)

0=s_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm;\ell}=g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-1).\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad g_{i,j;\ell}^{-}=\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-1).

(2)

If
\hat{b}_{i,n-1}+dm+\ell\notin\Delta
and
i
is not
\ell
-admissible, then necessarily
\ell\geq 2

since
\Delta
is admissible.
By
[

,
\hat{I}(i;\ell-1)\neq\emptyset
.
By
[

,
i\neq d-\ell
.
Hence,
g_{i;\ell-1}^{+}>g_{i+1;\ell-1}^{+}
.

(2.1)

If
\ell\geq 3
, then by (
3.14
), the new equation

0=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}/n}s_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm;\ell}
becomes

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda g_{i;\ell-1}^{+}-\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1\in\Delta}F_{i,j;1}\,g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1;\ell-1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell-1\in\Delta}F_{i,j;\ell-1}\,g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell-1;1}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-2).

Using
F_{i,j;\ell-1}=g_{i,j;\ell-1}^{-}+\lambda g_{\hat{b}_{i,j};\ell-2}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-3)
, this simplifies to

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda g_{i;\ell-1}^{+}-\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1\in\Delta}F_{i,j;1}\,g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1;\ell-1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell-1\in\Delta}g_{i,j;\ell-1}^{-}\,g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell-1;1}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-2).

(2.1.1)

We simplify further the expression
\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1\in\Delta}F_{i,j;1}\,g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1;\ell-1}
.

Since
\Delta
is admissible,
I(i;1)\subsetneq\mathbb{Z}/n
, hence

.

•

For

where

depends only on
i
5
5

5

Precisely,

if

; if

,

.
.
Then by (

‣
3.2
),

g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1;\ell-1}=g_{\hat{b}_{i+1,j+1+j_{1}};\ell-1}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-2).

Since

, we conclude

.

•

For

, we use (1):

As a consequence, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1\in\Delta}F_{i,j;1}g_{\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1;\ell-1}=\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1\in\Delta}F_{i,j;1}g_{\hat{b}_{i+1,j+1+j_{1}};\ell-1}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j:\penalty\ \hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+1\in\Delta}F_{i,j;1}(g_{\hat{b}_{i+1,j+1+j_{1}};\ell-1}-\frac{g_{i+1;\ell-1}^{+}}{N_{i+1;\ell-1}})+\frac{n\lambda}{N_{i+1;\ell-1}}g_{i+1;\ell-1}^{+}+\mathrm{Poly}(\leq\ell-2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n\lambda}{N_{i+1;\ell-1}}g_{i+1;\ell-1}^{+}+\mathrm{Poly}(<g_{d-\ell;\ell-1}^{+}).\quad(g_{\hat{b}_{i+1,j+1+j_{1}};\ell-1}-\frac{g_{i+1;\ell-1}^{+}}{N_{i+1;\ell-1}}\in\mathrm{Gen}_{i+1;\ell-1}^{-})

We simplify further the expression

If
\exists j

)-admissible, and

•

, we may write

,

, where

6
6

6

Precisely,

.
. Then by (

‣

),

, i.e.,

\forall j\in\hat{I}(i;\ell-1)^{c}

, we may write
\hat{b}_{i,j}+\ell-1=\hat{b}_{i+\ell-1,j+j_{\ell-1}}+\tilde{\gamma}_{i,j}^{(\ell-1)}dn
,

.
It follows that
\hat{b}_{i,j}+dm+\ell=\hat{b}_{i+\ell-1,j+j_{\ell-1}}+\tilde{\gamma}_{i,j}^{(\ell-1)}dn+dm+1\notin\Delta
